119	\paragraph{Feature space divergence.}
120	SHAP TreeExplainer analysis confirms that perplexity and surprisal features account for 88.8\% of RF's total contribution.
121	Three independent importance measures---SHAP, Gini, and permutation---show strong concordance (Kendall's $W{=}0.90$, $p{<}0.005$; pairwise Spearman $\rho$: SHAP--Gini $0.93$, SHAP--permutation $0.84$; Appendix~\ref{app:permutation}), with \texttt{p90\_ppl} appearing in the SHAP top-2 in 9/9 cells and \texttt{var\_surprisal} in 8/9.
122	Linear probing of PLM [CLS] representations yields negative $R^2$ for both \texttt{var\_surprisal} and \texttt{p90\_ppl} (Appendix~\ref{app:feature_overlap}), indicating that pretrained representations lack the linear structure needed to recover perplexity tail statistics---a systematic representational gap rather than a backbone-specific artifact.
123	
124	DCOL \cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\% (Appendix~\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\%) underperform feature-based methods, suggesting depth estimation benefits from targeted statistical features that capture distributional tail properties absent from PLM representations.
125	This comparison is bounded by backbone scale ($\leq$125M parameters); whether larger PLMs (e.g., 7B+) can close the gap via richer internal representations remains an open question.
126	
127	\subsection{Temperature Robustness}
128	\label{sec:gen:temperature}
129	
130	The cross-strategy results in \S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy} use a single temperature ($\tau{=}0.9$).
131	To test whether depth signal generalizes across temperatures, we repeat the classification pipeline at $\tau \in \{0.5, 0.7, 0.9, 1.1\}$ on Pythia-1.4B (full tables in Appendix~\ref{app:multi_tau}).
132	
133	\paragraph{Within-$\tau$ robustness.}
134	For $\tau \in [0.5, 0.9]$, d1-vs-d2 AUC exceeds 0.80 (peak 0.851 at $\tau{=}0.7$, comparable to nucleus $p{=}0.95$'s 0.861) and 4-class accuracy ranges from 75.4\% to 77.3\%, confirming the depth signal is not an artifact of a single temperature.
135	The same top-5 features (\texttt{var\_surprisal}, \texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{mean\_ppl}, \texttt{mean\_surprisal}, \texttt{p75\_ppl}) dominate across all $\tau$ values with only minor rank-order variation.
136	
137	\paragraph{Collapse at $\tau{=}1.1$: memorization as prerequisite.}
138	At $\tau{=}1.1$, 4-class accuracy drops to 57.2\% and d1v2 AUC falls to 0.528.
139	Training loss reveals the mechanism: at low temperature ($\tau{=}0.5$), fine-tuning loss decreases steeply across recursive depths ($2.27 \to 0.42 \to 0.16$), indicating rapid memorization of the model's own output.
140	At $\tau{=}1.1$, loss remains nearly flat ($2.27 \to 1.91 \to 1.85$)---high-entropy output resists memorization, so depth-dependent distributional shifts do not accumulate.
141	This establishes \emph{memorization as a prerequisite} for detectable depth signal: recursive depth is discriminable only when successive fine-tuning iterations progressively reshape the output distribution.
142	
143	\paragraph{Cross-$\tau$ non-transferability.}
144	Leave-one-$\tau$-out transfer yields mean accuracy of 46.7\% with mean d1v2 AUC of 0.319---worse than cross-strategy transfer (59.1\%; \S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).
145	Holdout $\tau{=}0.5$ produces inverted d1v2 AUC (0.176), confirming that different temperatures produce surprisal distributions with incompatible shapes.
146	This extends the strategy-specificity finding: even within a single decoding family, the feature space is temperature-specific, reinforcing the need for strategy-conditioned classifiers.
147	
148	\paragraph{Ecological validity.}
149	On WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages), all three model-specific RF classifiers achieve $\geq$98.5\% d0 accuracy despite $+$621\% domain shift in mean perplexity---partly an easy case, as WikiText's higher perplexity places it farther from d1--d3 in feature space.
150	On noisier web-crawl data (5{,}000 C4 passages), FPR ranges from 4.7\% (OLMo) to 22.4\% (GPT-2~XL), concentrated in template/SEO content with high n-gram repetition (Table~\ref{tab:calibration}); deployment on heterogeneous web data requires model-specific calibration.
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	We formalized recursive generation depth estimation and showed that a compact feature set dominated by perplexity tail statistics achieves 70--85\% four-class accuracy ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ across models; \S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}), with five features sufficing for 97.6\% of full performance and classifier-agnostic convergence within $\leq$0.4pp.
5	The dominant pair (\texttt{p90\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9 cells, \texttt{var\_surprisal} in 8/9 under SHAP) generalizes across the three studied model families without retraining, making depth estimation a candidate early-warning signal for model collapse~\cite{shumailov2024_nature} within this experimental scope.
6	Recent work corroborates this connection, identifying entropy decline as a measurable signal of recursive degradation that parallels the tail erosion our features capture~\cite{shi2025_closer_look}.
7	Zero-shot LLM baselines reinforce this view: DeepSeek V3.2 (36.2\%) and Qwen2.5-7B (30.4\%) exhibit complementary failure modes---the former predicts nearly all samples as d0 while the latter is biased toward d1---yet both perform near chance, confirming that depth discrimination requires the targeted statistical features our pipeline extracts rather than general language understanding.
8	
9	Cross-strategy transfer remains the primary bottleneck (59.1\% vs.\ 66.4\% cross-model), with the temperature-induced d1/d2 AUC inversion making single-classifier deployment across unknown decoding strategies infeasible.
10	A multi-temperature ablation ($\tau \in [0.5, 1.1]$, Pythia-1.4B; \S\ref{sec:gen:temperature}) reinforces this: within-$\tau$ depth discrimination is robust for $\tau \leq 0.9$ (d1v2 AUC $>0.80$), but cross-$\tau$ transfer (mean 46.7\%) is harder than cross-strategy transfer (59.1\%), confirming that strategy-conditioned classifiers are necessary.
11	A two-stage pipeline---first identifying the decoding strategy, then applying a strategy-conditioned depth classifier---could address this.
12	Different strategies produce distinguishable perplexity signatures (top-$k$ yields narrower distributions; temperature sampling produces heavier tails), suggesting that automatic strategy identification is technically feasible, though independent validation on held-out strategy sets is needed.
13	As an alternative, when deployment is restricted to a single platform or API provider, the decoding strategy is typically known a priori, and a strategy-conditioned classifier can be applied directly.
14	
15	\paragraph{Depth-graduated data curation.}
16	Current data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction~\cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.
17	Depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.
18	As a proof of concept, we fine-tune Pythia-410M with LoRA ($r{=}16$) for 3 epochs on subsets selected by RF-predicted depth, random subsampling, or oracle depth labels (8 conditions, 5 seeds each; Table~\ref{tab:curation_poc}).
19	RF depth-based filtering significantly outperforms size-matched random subsampling at all three stringency tiers (strict 25\%: $\Delta{=}0.43$ PPL, $p{=}0.002$; moderate 50\%: $\Delta{=}0.24$, $p{=}0.02$; soft 44\%: $\Delta{=}0.33$, $p{<}0.001$; paired $t$-tests), and the soft-threshold RF condition achieves perplexity statistically indistinguishable from unfiltered training on the full 10{,}000 samples while using only 44\% of the data ($p{=}0.41$).
20	Interestingly, oracle-strict filtering does not improve over unfiltered ($p{=}0.22$), suggesting that classifier imprecision preserves beneficial data diversity that pure depth thresholding removes.
21	This PoC operates within a single known strategy (Pythia nucleus); cross-strategy deployment would first require strategy identification to avoid the d1/d2 boundary inversion (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}), tying curation accuracy to the two-stage pipeline discussed above.
22	Importance weighting~\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding, and verification-guided retraining offers a complementary mitigation path~\cite{yi2026_escaping_collapse}.
23	Full per-condition statistics appear in Appendix~\ref{app:curation_poc}.
24	
25	\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}
26	Depth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.
27	Watermarking~\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.
28	Source attribution~\cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.
29	Bevendorff et al.\ distinguish attribution from verification as two fundamental detection paradigms~\cite{bevendorff2025_two_paradigms}; depth estimation represents a complementary third axis that characterizes \emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.
30	A layered provenance system combining watermark presence, source model, and recursive depth could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.
31	
32	\paragraph{Mixed-depth documents.}
33	Real-world corpora often interleave segments at different recursive depths within a single document.
34	Sliding-window depth estimation at the segment level offers a natural extension: windows of 256--512 tokens preserve sufficient statistical power for tail feature computation while enabling sub-document depth maps.
35	Such maps could inform paragraph-level data curation, selectively retaining low-depth segments even from partially contaminated documents, and would complement document-level classifiers that flag mixed-provenance content for manual review.
36	
37	\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}
38	Adversarial post-processing---paraphrasing, back-translation, or targeted lexical substitution---poses a natural threat to depth estimation.
39	Because perplexity tail statistics reflect distributional properties of the recursive generation process rather than surface lexical patterns, they exhibit inherent resistance to shallow transformations that preserve the underlying surprisal structure.
40	However, robustness to targeted attacks that explicitly optimize the output perplexity distribution---e.g., iterative rewriting guided by tail-statistic objectives---remains unverified, and attack-aware features (syntactic complexity, discourse coherence markers) may be necessary for deployment in adversarial settings.
41	
42	\paragraph{Scaling predictions.}
43	All current experiments use 1--1.5B parameter models; larger models (7B+) generate more fluent text with narrower perplexity distributions, potentially compressing inter-depth separability.
44	However, based on the mechanism identified at the 1--1.5B scale studied here, recursive training amplifies distributional artifacts because tail erosion is driven by retraining on one's own output distribution, not by model capacity per se.
45	We therefore expect depth estimation to remain feasible at larger scales, though recalibration of feature thresholds and possibly richer feature sets (e.g., attention entropy or generation diversity metrics) will likely be required.
46	
47	\paragraph{Limitations and future directions.}
48	Real-world documents may interleave passages at mixed recursive depths within a single document; our passage-level classifier assumes uniform depth, and extending to segment-level estimation introduces a granularity--statistical-power tradeoff that remains unexplored.
49	Web-crawl deployment requires model-specific calibration (C4 FPR: 4.7--22.4\%; FineWeb corroborates this at 19.5\% FPR within $<$1pp of C4, confirming cross-dataset consistency; Table~\ref{tab:calibration}), and all experiments use 1--1.5B models.
50	Adversarial robustness is only partially characterized: tail-based features resist surface-level paraphrasing due to their dependence on deep distributional structure, but targeted distribution-aware attacks remain untested.
51	Future work should validate on mixed-domain corpora at larger scales and evaluate whether depth-graduated curation, with appropriate strategy conditioning, improves downstream training outcomes.
52	
```

\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}), with five features sufficing for 97.6\\% of full performance and classifier-agnostic convergence within $\\leq$0.4pp.\nThe dominant pair (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9 cells, \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9 under SHAP) generalizes across the three studied model families without retraining, making depth estimation a candidate early-warning signal for model collapse~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature} within this experimental scope.\nRecent work corroborates this connection, identifying entropy decline as a measurable signal of recursive degradation that parallels the tail erosion our features capture~\\cite{shi2025_closer_look}.\nZero-shot LLM baselines reinforce this view: DeepSeek V3.2 (36.2\\%) and Qwen2.5-7B (30.4\\%) exhibit complementary failure modes---the former predicts nearly all samples as d0 while the latter is biased toward d1---yet both perform near chance, confirming that depth discrimination requires the targeted statistical features our pipeline extracts rather than general language understanding.\n\nCross-strategy transfer remains the primary bottleneck (59.1\\% vs.\\ 66.4\\% cross-model), with the temperature-induced d1/d2 AUC inversion making single-classifier deployment across unknown decoding strategies infeasible.\nA multi-temperature ablation ($\\tau \\in [0.5, 1.1]$, Pythia-1.4B; \\S\\ref{sec:gen:temperature}) reinforces this: within-$\\tau$ depth discrimination is robust for $\\tau \\leq 0.9$ (d1v2 AUC $>0.80$), but cross-$\\tau$ transfer (mean 46.7\\%) is harder than cross-strategy transfer (59.1\\%), confirming that strategy-conditioned classifiers are necessary.\nA two-stage pipeline---first identifying the decoding strategy, then applying a strategy-conditioned depth classifier---could address this.\nDifferent strategies produce distinguishable perplexity signatures (top-$k$ yields narrower distributions; temperature sampling produces heavier tails), suggesting that automatic strategy identification is technically feasible, though independent validation on held-out strategy sets is needed.\nAs an alternative, when deployment is restricted to a single platform or API provider, the decoding strategy is typically known a priori, and a strategy-conditioned classifier can be applied directly.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth-graduated data curation.}\nCurrent data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction~\\cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.\nDepth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.\nAs a proof of concept, we fine-tune Pythia-410M with LoRA ($r{=}16$) for 3 epochs on subsets selected by RF-predicted depth, random subsampling, or oracle depth labels (8 conditions, 5 seeds each; Table~\\ref{tab:curation_poc}).\nRF depth-based filtering significantly outperforms size-matched random subsampling at all three stringency tiers (strict 25\\%: $\\Delta{=}0.43$ PPL, $p{=}0.002$; moderate 50\\%: $\\Delta{=}0.24$, $p{=}0.02$; soft 44\\%: $\\Delta{=}0.33$, $p{<}0.001$; paired $t$-tests), and the soft-threshold RF condition achieves perplexity statistically indistinguishable from unfiltered training on the full 10{,}000 samples while using only 44\\% of the data ($p{=}0.41$).\nInterestingly, oracle-strict filtering does not improve over unfiltered ($p{=}0.22$), suggesting that classifier imprecision preserves beneficial data diversity that pure depth thresholding removes.\nThis PoC operates within a single known strategy (Pythia nucleus); cross-strategy deployment would first require strategy identification to avoid the d1/d2 boundary inversion (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}), tying curation accuracy to the two-stage pipeline discussed above.\nImportance weighting~\\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding, and verification-guided retraining offers a complementary mitigation path~\\cite{yi2026_escaping_collapse}.\nFull per-condition statistics appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:curation_poc}.\n\n\\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}\nDepth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.\nWatermarking~\\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.\nSource attribution~\\cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.\nBevendorff et al.\\ distinguish attribution from verification as two fundamental detection paradigms~\\cite{bevendorff2025_two_paradigms}; depth estimation represents a complementary third axis that characterizes \\emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.\nA layered provenance system combining watermark presence, source model, and recursive depth could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.\n\n\\paragraph{Mixed-depth documents.}\nReal-world corpora often interleave segments at different recursive depths within a single document.\nSliding-window depth estimation at the segment level offers a natural extension: windows of 256--512 tokens preserve sufficient statistical power for tail feature computation while enabling sub-document depth maps.\nSuch maps could inform paragraph-level data curation, selectively retaining low-depth segments even from partially contaminated documents, and would complement document-level classifiers that flag mixed-provenance content for manual review.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}\nAdversarial post-processing---paraphrasing, back-translation, or targeted lexical substitution---poses a natural threat to depth estimation.\nBecause perplexity tail statistics reflect distributional properties of the recursive generation process rather than surface lexical patterns, they exhibit inherent resistance to shallow transformations that preserve the underlying surprisal structure.\nHowever, robustness to targeted attacks that explicitly optimize the output perplexity distribution---e.g., iterative rewriting guided by tail-statistic objectives---remains unverified, and attack-aware features (syntactic complexity, discourse coherence markers) may be necessary for deployment in adversarial settings.\n\n\\paragraph{Scaling predictions.}\nAll current experiments use 1--1.5B parameter models; larger models (7B+) generate more fluent text with narrower perplexity distributions, potentially compressing inter-depth separability.\nHowever, based on the mechanism identified at the 1--1.5B scale studied here, recursive training amplifies distributional artifacts because tail erosion is driven by retraining on one's own output distribution, not by model capacity per se.\nWe therefore expect depth estimation to remain feasible at larger scales, though recalibration of feature thresholds and possibly richer feature sets (e.g., attention entropy or generation diversity metrics) will likely be required.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations and future directions.}\nReal-world documents may interleave passages at mixed recursive depths within a single document; our passage-level classifier assumes uniform depth, and extending to segment-level estimation introduces a granularity--statistical-power tradeoff that remains unexplored.\nWeb-crawl deployment requires model-specific calibration (C4 FPR: 4.7--22.4\\%; FineWeb corroborates this at 19.5\\% FPR within $<$1pp of C4, confirming cross-dataset consistency; Table~\\ref{tab:calibration}), and all experiments use 1--1.5B models.\nAdversarial robustness is only partially characterized: tail-based features resist surface-level paraphrasing due to their dependence on deep distributional structure, but targeted distribution-aware attacks remain untested.\nFuture work should validate on mixed-domain corpora at larger scales and evaluate whether depth-graduated curation, with appropriate strategy conditioning, improves downstream training outcomes.\n",
      "numLines": 52,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 52
    }
  }
}

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}
5	Cross-strategy transfer induces a d1/d2 boundary inversion under temperature sampling.
6	Per-strategy z-score normalization partially corrects the inversion under logistic regression (GPT-2~XL d1v2 AUC $0.24{\to}0.56$, OLMo $0.19{\to}0.70$) but yields negligible improvement for the Random Forest classifier used throughout the main experiments ($\Delta < 0.02$\,pp), and even in the best case (logistic regression) leaves a residual gap relative to within-strategy performance (${\sim}0.83$), indicating that a two-stage pipeline (strategy identification + strategy-conditioned classification) is needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.
7	
8	\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}
9	Depth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$98\% across all nine model--strategy settings), inflating headline accuracy.
10	Cross-model transfer accuracy of 66.4\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).
11	
12	\paragraph{Controlled vs.\ ecological setting.}
13	All experiments use Wikipedia seed text with controlled recursive generation (LoRA fine-tuning, 1--1.5B parameter models).
14	This protocol isolates the recursive depth variable but diverges from wild recursive contamination, where models are pretrained on mixed corpora containing prior-generation outputs at unknown proportions; the fingerprints we identify may attenuate or shift under such diffuse exposure.
15	Depth signal is the dominant classification factor (5-fold CV 59--82\%, up to 2.5$\times$ chance): LOGO accuracy (72.1\%) matches standard CV (72.0\%), and seed-group prediction is at chance (10.2\% vs.\ 10\%; Appendix~\ref{app:domain_bias}).
16	Transfer to instruction-tuned models and larger scales (7B+) remains open.
17	Real-world documents may also interleave segments at different recursive generation depths; extending to segment-level estimation introduces a granularity--statistical-power tradeoff.
18	
19	\paragraph{Domain-dependent false positive rate.}
20	On editorial text (WikiText-103), all classifiers achieve $\geq$98.5\% d0 accuracy, but on web-crawl data (C4, 5{,}000 passages), FPR ranges from 4.7\% (OLMo) to 22.4\% (GPT-2~XL).
21	Misclassifications concentrate in template/SEO content with elevated repetition features (\texttt{rep\_3gram} $+$37\%, \texttt{rep\_4gram} $+$75\% vs.\ correctly classified d0); the model gap likely reflects training data composition (OLMo's Dolma includes diverse web data vs.\ Pythia/GPT-2~XL's Wikipedia/WebText).
22	Deployment on heterogeneous web corpora requires model- and domain-specific calibration.
23	
24	\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}
25	Adversarial paraphrasing \cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.
26	
27	\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}
28	Our EDD definition (Eq.~\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically---as observed in all our configurations.
29	However, \citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).
30	Our cross-strategy transfer results provide an empirical example: training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1-vs-d2 AUC as low as 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo), violating the monotonicity assumption.
31	In such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.
32	The choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.
33	
34	\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}
35	Surprisal-based features are computed relative to GPT-2 base (124M), introducing partial overlap when GPT-2~XL is held out, though GPT-2~XL holdout accuracy (67.6\%) does not exceed the unrelated OLMo (69.2\%).
36	The choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.
37	
38	\paragraph{Additional caveats.}
39	Detection features degrade under domain shift \cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}; our depth features are likely susceptible.
40	\citet{falahati2026_curated} show that curated synthetic data need not trigger collapse, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.
41	
42	\section*{Ethics Statement}
43	
44	This work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.
45	No human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.
46	Our method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.
47	Code and data will be released upon acceptance.
```

1	\section{Additional Results}
2	\label{app:additional}
3	
4	\subsection{Top-$k$ Feature Trajectories}
5	\label{app:topk_trajectories}
6	
7	Figure~\ref{fig:feature_trajectory} in the main text shows feature trajectories for nucleus ($p{=}0.95$) and temperature ($\tau{=}0.9$) sampling.
8	Top-$k$ ($k{=}50$) trajectories follow the same monotonically decreasing trend for all features but with compressed inter-depth spacing, consistent with the signal attenuation effect discussed in \S\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}.
9	Concretely, the d0$\to$d1 drop in \textsc{p90\_ppl} under top-$k$ is 78\% (vs.\ 87\% for nucleus), and the d2$\to$d3 reduction is 31\% (vs.\ 39\% for nucleus).
10	Despite the magnitude reduction, feature rank ordering across depths is preserved, which explains why ordinal classifiers maintain directionality even under top-$k$ conditions.
11	
12	\subsection{Permutation Importance}
13	\label{app:permutation}
14	
15	To verify that the Gini-based importance rankings in \S\ref{sec:atlas:features} are not an artifact of the impurity measure, we compute permutation importance (5-fold CV, 10 repeats) for all 9 atlas cells.
16	The top-2 features are stable across measures: \textsc{p90\_ppl} ranks in the top 2 in 9/9 cells under both Gini and permutation importance, and \textsc{var\_surprisal} in 8/9 (vs.\ 7/9 for Gini, with the additional cell being OLMo top-$k$; the single SHAP exception is GPT-2~XL top-$k$, where \textsc{p75\_ppl} displaces \textsc{var\_surprisal} to rank 3).
17	Kendall's $W{=}0.90$ across all three methods (Gini, permutation, SHAP) indicates strong concordance ($p{<}0.005$).
18	Pairwise Spearman correlations are: SHAP--Gini $\rho{=}0.93$, SHAP--permutation $\rho{=}0.84$, Gini--permutation $\rho{=}0.76$.
19	Per-cell top-5 overlap between SHAP and permutation importance is 3--4 out of 5 features; the aggregated (mean-across-cells) top-5 sets are identical, confirming that the top features are robust to the choice of importance measure at the aggregate level while per-cell rankings exhibit model-dependent variation.
20	
21	\subsection{Per-Class Recall}
22	\label{app:per_class}
23	
24	Table~\ref{tab:per_class_recall} reports per-class recall across all 9 atlas cells (4-class in-distribution, 5-fold CV).
25	Depth~0 recall exceeds 98\% uniformly.
26	Among generated depths, d2 is the hardest class in every cell; the deficit is most pronounced under top-$k$ sampling (46--53\%), where vocabulary truncation removes the tail tokens that carry depth-discriminative signal.
27	
28	\begin{table}[h]
29	\centering
30	\small
31	\caption{Per-class recall (\%) for all 9 atlas cells. d2 is the lowest-recall class in every cell. Boldface marks the highest d2 recall.}
32	\label{tab:per_class_recall}
33	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
34	\toprule
35	Cell & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\
36	\midrule
37	Pythia, nuc.       & 99.7 & 81.9 & 58.7 & 72.3 \\
38	Pythia, $\tau$     & 99.7 & 81.3 & 53.9 & 72.1 \\
39	Pythia, top-$k$    & 99.5 & 72.3 & 45.6 & 57.5 \\
40	OLMo, nuc.         & 98.8 & 80.5 & 61.5 & 74.3 \\
41	OLMo, $\tau$       & 99.7 & 87.7 & 66.1 & 77.2 \\
42	OLMo, top-$k$      & 99.5 & 78.5 & 51.2 & 65.6 \\
43	GPT-2~XL, nuc.     & 99.2 & 85.9 & \textbf{73.3} & 77.5 \\
44	GPT-2~XL, $\tau$   & 99.5 & 90.9 & 68.8 & 81.9 \\
45	GPT-2~XL, top-$k$  & 98.5 & 79.5 & 52.5 & 71.1 \\
46	\bottomrule
47	\end{tabular}
48	\end{table}
49	
50	\subsection{DCOL Iteration Details}
51	\label{app:dcol_iterations}
52	
53	Table~\ref{tab:dcol_iterations} summarizes all six DCOL iterations.
54	DeBERTa-v3-large exhibited systematic numerical instability across five configurations; RoBERTa-large resolved stability but could not close the accuracy gap with feature-based methods.
55	
56	\begin{table}[h]
57	\centering
58	\small
59	\caption{DCOL iteration summary. All experiments use Pythia-1.4B nucleus data. RF baseline: 78.2\%.}
60	\label{tab:dcol_iterations}
61	\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}
62	\toprule
63	Ver. & Backbone & Config & Outcome \\
64	\midrule
65	v1 & DeBERTa & FT, m\_lr $10^{-3}$ & Embed.\ collapse (31.5\%) \\
66	v2 & DeBERTa & FT, m\_lr $10^{-4}$ & OOM \\
67	v3 & DeBERTa & FT, m\_lr $10^{-4}$ & Forgetting + OOM \\
68	v4 & DeBERTa & LoRA, m\_lr $10^{-3}$ & Embed.\ collapse \\
69	v5 & DeBERTa & LoRA, m\_lr $10^{-4}$ & NaN gradients \\
70	v6 & RoBERTa & FT, grad clip 1.0 & Stable; 64.5\% ($-$13.7pp) \\
71	\bottomrule
72	\end{tabular}

74	
75	\subsection{Zero-Shot LLM Prompt Template}
76	\label{app:zeroshot_prompt}
77	
78	Both DeepSeek V3.2 and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct use identical prompting.
79	The system message defines recursive generation depth with per-depth descriptions (depth~0: human-written; depth~1--3: successive LLM generation cycles) and lists expected textual patterns at each depth (vocabulary richness, repetition, coherence).
80	The user message presents the passage (truncated to ${\approx}$384 words) and requests a JSON response containing an integer depth prediction (0--3), a confidence score, and a one-sentence reasoning.
81	No in-context examples are provided.
82	
83	\begin{quote}
84	\small
85	\textbf{System:} ``You are an expert at detecting AI-generated text and estimating its generation depth. `Recursive generation depth' refers to how many times a text has been through an LLM generation cycle: Depth~0: Original human-written text [\ldots] Depth~3: Text generated by an LLM given depth-2 text as input. Key patterns by depth: Depth~0 (human): Rich vocabulary [\ldots] Depth~3 (third-gen): Noticeable repetition, formulaic structure [\ldots]''
86	
87	\textbf{User:} ``Analyze the following text and estimate its recursive generation depth (0, 1, 2, or 3). TEXT: \{text\}. Respond with ONLY a JSON object: \{\texttt{"depth"}: $\langle$int 0--3$\rangle$, \texttt{"confidence"}: $\langle$float 0--1$\rangle$, \texttt{"reasoning"}: $\langle$str$\rangle$\}''
88	\end{quote}
89	
90	\subsection{Multi-Temperature Ablation}
91	\label{app:multi_tau}
92	
93	To test whether the depth signal is an artifact of a single temperature setting, we repeat the within-strategy classification pipeline at four temperatures $\tau \in \{0.5, 0.7, 0.9, 1.1\}$ using Pythia-1.4B.
94	
95	\paragraph{Within-$\tau$ classification.}
96	Table~\ref{tab:multi_tau_within} reports 4-class accuracy and pairwise depth AUCs for each temperature.
97	For $\tau \in [0.5, 0.9]$, d1-vs-d2 AUC exceeds 0.80 and 4-class accuracy ranges from 75.4\% to 77.3\%, confirming that the depth signal is not specific to $\tau{=}0.9$.
98	At $\tau{=}1.1$, accuracy drops to 57.2\% and d1v2 AUC falls to 0.528.
99	Training loss provides a mechanistic account: at $\tau{=}0.5$, loss decreases from 2.27 to 0.16 over three recursive iterations (rapid memorization), whereas at $\tau{=}1.1$ loss remains at 1.85 after the same iterations---the model fails to memorize its own high-entropy output, and depth-dependent distributional shifts do not accumulate.
100	
101	\begin{table}[h]
102	\centering
103	\small
104	\caption{Within-$\tau$ 4-class classification (Pythia-1.4B, 5-fold CV). d$i$v$j$: pairwise AUC for depth $i$ vs.\ depth $j$.}
105	\label{tab:multi_tau_within}
106	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
107	\toprule
108	$\tau$ & 4-class acc (\%) & d1v2 AUC & d0v1 AUC & d2v3 AUC \\
109	\midrule
110	0.5 & 77.3 & 0.842 & 0.996 & 0.764 \\
111	0.7 & 75.4 & 0.851 & 0.997 & 0.720 \\
112	0.9 & 75.9 & 0.808 & 0.991 & 0.781 \\
113	1.1 & 57.2 & 0.528 & 0.988 & 0.599 \\
114	\bottomrule
115	\end{tabular}
116	\end{table}
117	
118	\paragraph{Cross-$\tau$ transfer.}
119	Leave-one-$\tau$-out evaluation (Table~\ref{tab:multi_tau_cross}) yields mean accuracy 46.7\% with d1v2 AUC as low as 0.176, worse than cross-strategy LOO (59.1\%; \S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).
120	Different temperatures produce surprisal distributions with distinct shapes, making cross-$\tau$ transfer harder than cross-strategy transfer despite both involving the same model.
121	
122	\begin{table}[h]
123	\centering
124	\small
125	\caption{Cross-$\tau$ leave-one-out transfer (Pythia-1.4B). Train on three temperatures, test on the held-out one.}
126	\label{tab:multi_tau_cross}
127	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
128	\toprule
129	Holdout & Acc (\%) & d1v2 AUC \\
130	\midrule
131	$\tau{=}0.5$ & 52.2 & 0.176 \\
132	$\tau{=}0.7$ & 41.0 & 0.299 \\
133	$\tau{=}0.9$ & 46.4 & 0.277 \\
134	$\tau{=}1.1$ & 47.1 & 0.525 \\
135	\midrule
136	Mean & 46.7 & 0.319 \\
137	\bottomrule
138	\end{tabular}
139	\end{table}
140	
141	These results are based on a single model family (Pythia-1.4B); multi-model confirmation remains open.
142	
143	\subsection{Feature Overlap Analysis}
144	\label{app:feature_overlap}
145	
146	We probe whether end-to-end PLM representations capture the same depth signals as our statistical features via linear probing of [CLS] representations from DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base.
147	PLM representations excel at surface lexical features (TTR: $R^2{=}0.85$, repeated bigrams) but fail to capture perplexity distribution statistics---\textsc{var\_surprisal} and \textsc{p90\_ppl} yield negative $R^2$ under linear probing, indicating these features are not linearly accessible in the pretrained representation space.
148	This confirms that the 19pp accuracy gap between feature-based and end-to-end methods (\S\ref{sec:gen:e2e}) stems from complementary---not redundant---representations: PLMs capture lexical patterns while the targeted statistical features capture distributional tail properties that constitute the dominant depth markers.
149	
150	\subsection{Domain Bias Analysis}
151	\label{app:domain_bias}
152	
153	To assess whether the classifier exploits depth-related features or memorizes stylistic cues from individual seed articles, we conduct three sub-experiments.
154	
155	\paragraph{Methodological note.}
156	An initial implementation assigned sample IDs with a global counter across depths, inadvertently correlating seed-group identity with depth (LOGO accuracy 64.0\%, seed-group prediction 20.8\%).
157	We discovered the issue during internal review, corrected to per-depth seed assignment ensuring each group contains equal samples per depth, and re-ran all sub-experiments.
158	The results below use the corrected pipeline; the initial (buggy) numbers are reported for transparency.
159	
160	\paragraph{Sub-A: Standard CV.}
161	Five-fold CV accuracy on 3-class (d1/d2/d3) classification ranges from 59\% to 82\% across the 9 atlas cells (up to 2.5$\times$ above the 33.3\% chance level), confirming that depth signal is the dominant classification factor.
162	
163	\paragraph{Sub-B: LOGO evaluation.}
164	We assign each passage to one of 10 seed-article groups (balanced: each group contains equal samples per depth) and perform leave-one-group-out CV.
165	LOGO mean accuracy is 72.1\% (range 59.5--81.6\%), matching standard CV (72.0\%) within noise (before fix: 64.0\%, reflecting the depth--group confound).
166	Per-cell fold standard deviations range from 0.4pp to 1.4pp, indicating that cross-group generalization is as reliable as within-group classification.
167	
168	\paragraph{Sub-C: Negative control.}
169	We train two classifiers on identical features: one predicting depth, the other predicting seed-group identity (10-class, chance = 10\%).
170	Depth prediction accuracy averages 72.0\%; seed-group prediction averages 10.2\%---indistinguishable from chance (before fix: 20.8\%, inflated by the depth--group confound).
171	The depth-to-seed-group accuracy ratio is 7.1$\times$, confirming that the feature representation encodes depth but not seed-article identity.
172	
173	Taken together, these results show that seed-article grouping has no measurable effect on depth estimation after correcting the group assignment: LOGO accuracy equals standard CV, and seed-group prediction is at chance.
174	
175	\subsection{Wild-Data Calibration}
176	\label{app:calibration}
177	
178	Table~\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages).
179	
180	\begin{table}[h]
181	\centering
182	\small
183	\caption{Wild-data d0 accuracy and FPR for nucleus RF classifiers. All passages are human-written (true d0). FPR = fraction misclassified as d1+.}
184	\label{tab:calibration}
185	\begin{tabular}{@{}llrcc@{}}
186	\toprule
187	Model & Source & $n$ & d0 (\%) & FPR (\%) \\
188	\midrule
189	Pythia-1.4B & WikiText & 546 & 100.0 & 0.0 \\
190	 & C4 & 5{,}000 & 79.8 & 20.2 \\
191	\addlinespace
192	GPT-2~XL & WikiText & 546 & 99.3 & 0.7 \\
193	 & C4 & 5{,}000 & 77.6 & 22.4 \\
194	\addlinespace
195	OLMo-1B & WikiText & 546 & 98.5 & 1.5 \\
196	 & C4 & 5{,}000 & 95.3 & 4.7 \\
197	\bottomrule
198	\end{tabular}
199	\end{table}
200	
201	On WikiText-103, all three classifiers achieve $\geq$98.5\% d0 accuracy, confirming that editorial text is reliably distinguished from recursive generations across model families.
202	On C4, FPR varies sharply: OLMo (4.7\%) vs.\ Pythia (20.2\%) and GPT-2~XL (22.4\%).
203	C4 misclassifications concentrate in template and SEO content with elevated repetition features (\texttt{rep\_3gram} $+$37\%, \texttt{rep\_4gram} $+$75\% vs.\ correctly classified d0 passages), rather than in random noise.
204	The model gap likely reflects training data composition: OLMo (trained on Dolma, which includes diverse web data) produces d0 features more tolerant of web-crawl variation, while Pythia and GPT-2~XL (trained primarily on Wikipedia/WebText) map template-heavy content into the d1+ region.
205	Among C4 false positives for GPT-2~XL, predictions distribute as d1 (9.0\%), d2 (3.7\%), d3 (9.6\%); for OLMo, d1 (2.9\%), d2 (0.5\%), d3 (1.3\%)---the non-uniform depth distribution suggests the classifier maps specific repetition patterns to particular depth signatures rather than producing random misclassifications.
206	
207	\subsection{Downstream Curation PoC}
208	\label{app:curation_poc}
209	
210	To validate that depth estimation translates to actionable data curation, we conduct a proof-of-concept downstream evaluation.
211	We fine-tune Pythia-410M with LoRA ($r{=}16$, $\alpha{=}32$) for 3 epochs on 8 data conditions derived from the Pythia nucleus depth-estimation corpus, repeating each condition over 5 random seeds.
212	Conditions vary the selection mechanism (RF-predicted depth, oracle depth, random subsampling) and stringency (strict $\approx$25\%, moderate $\approx$50\%, soft threshold).
213	Table~\ref{tab:curation_poc} reports per-condition perplexity; Table~\ref{tab:curation_comparisons} reports pairwise contrasts.
214

216	\centering

219	\label{tab:curation_poc}

253	
254	\subsection{Extended Discussion}
255	\label{app:extended_discussion}
256	
257	\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation.}
258	Current data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.
259	Recursive generation depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.
260	The 70--85\% in-distribution accuracy and $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ suggest depth-graduated filtering is feasible within known model--strategy configurations, though the d1/d2 boundary inversion under cross-strategy transfer (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) means reliable curation requires either known decoding strategy metadata or an upstream strategy-identification step; a small-scale PoC (Appendix~\ref{app:curation_poc}) shows RF-filtered subsets match unfiltered quality with 44\% of the data; large-scale validation remains open.
261	Importance weighting \cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.
262	
263	\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}
264	Depth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.
265	Watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.
266	Source attribution \cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.
267	Depth estimation characterizes \emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.
268	A layered provenance system combining these three signals---watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth---could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.
269	
```

</details>

seed-group prediction averages 10.2\\%---indistinguishable from chance (before fix: 20.8\\%, inflated by the depth--group confound).\nThe depth-to-seed-group accuracy ratio is 7.1$\\times$, confirming that the feature representation encodes depth but not seed-article identity.\n\nTaken together, these results show that seed-article grouping has no measurable effect on depth estimation after correcting the group assignment: LOGO accuracy equals standard CV, and seed-group prediction is at chance.\n\n\\subsection{Wild-Data Calibration}\n\\label{app:calibration}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Wild-data d0 accuracy and FPR for nucleus RF classifiers. All passages are human-written (true d0). FPR = fraction misclassified as d1+.}\n\\label{tab:calibration}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Source & $n$ & d0 (\\%) & FPR (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B & WikiText & 546 & 100.0 & 0.0 \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 79.8 & 20.2 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nGPT-2~XL & WikiText & 546 & 99.3 & 0.7 \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 77.6 & 22.4 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nOLMo-1B & WikiText & 546 & 98.5 & 1.5 \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 95.3 & 4.7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOn WikiText-103, all three classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, confirming that editorial text is reliably distinguished from recursive generations across model families.\nOn C4, FPR varies sharply: OLMo (4.7\\%) vs.\\ Pythia (20.2\\%) and GPT-2~XL (22.4\\%).\nC4 misclassifications concentrate in template and SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0 passages), rather than in random noise.\nThe model gap likely reflects training data composition: OLMo (trained on Dolma, which includes diverse web data) produces d0 features more tolerant of web-crawl variation, while Pythia and GPT-2~XL (trained primarily on Wikipedia/WebText) map template-heavy content into the d1+ region.\nAmong C4 false positives for GPT-2~XL, predictions distribute as d1 (9.0\\%), d2 (3.7\\%), d3 (9.6\\%); for OLMo, d1 (2.9\\%), d2 (0.5\\%), d3 (1.3\\%)---the non-uniform depth distribution suggests the classifier maps specific repetition patterns to particular depth signatures rather than producing random misclassifications.\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Curation PoC}\n\\label{app:curation_poc}\n\nTo validate that depth estimation translates to actionable data curation, we conduct a proof-of-concept downstream evaluation.\nWe fine-tune Pythia-410M with LoRA ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$) for 3 epochs on 8 data conditions derived from the Pythia nucleus depth-estimation corpus, repeating each condition over 5 random seeds.\nConditions vary the selection mechanism (RF-predicted depth, oracle depth, random subsampling) and stringency (strict $\\approx$25\\%, moderate $\\approx$50\\%, soft threshold).\nTable~\\ref{tab:curation_poc} reports per-condition perplexity; Table~\\ref{tab:curation_comparisons} reports pairwise contrasts.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Curation PoC: per-condition perplexity (Pythia-410M LoRA, 5 seeds). $\\Delta$ is signed so that positive values indicate lower (better) perplexity than unfiltered. $^*$\\,: $p<0.05$ (paired $t$-test vs.\\ unfiltered).}\n\\label{tab:curation_poc}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Mean PPL & Std & $n$ & $\\Delta$ vs Unfilt. & $p$ & $d_z$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nunfiltered        & 15.776 & 0.066 & 10{,}000 & ---      & ---   & ---      \\\\\nrf\\_soft          & 15.733 & 0.038 &  4{,}422 & +0.043   & 0.407 &  0.41    \\\\\nrf\\_moderate      & 15.756 & 0.056 &  4{,}940 & +0.021   & 0.492 &  0.34    \\\\\nrf\\_strict        & 15.810 & 0.061 &  2{,}496 & $-$0.034 & 0.607 & $-$0.25  \\\\\noracle\\_moderate  & 15.809 & 0.103 &  5{,}000 & $-$0.033 & 0.485 & $-$0.34  \\\\\noracle\\_strict    & 15.879 & 0.128 &  2{,}500 & $-$0.103 & 0.216 & $-$0.66  \\\\\nrandom\\_soft      & 16.062 & 0.079 &  4{,}422 & $-$0.286 & ${<}$0.001$^*$ & $-$2.77  \\\\\nrandom\\_moderate  & 15.998 & 0.090 &  4{,}940 & $-$0.222 & 0.028$^*$ & $-$1.50  \\\\\nrandom\\_strict    & 16.239 & 0.165 &  2{,}496 & $-$0.463 & 0.013$^*$ & $-$1.89  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Pairwise comparisons (paired $t$-test, 5 seeds). $^*$\\,: $p<0.05$; $^{**}$\\,: $p<0.01$.}\n\\label{tab:curation_comparisons}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nComparison & $\\Delta$ PPL & $t$ & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nrf\\_strict vs random\\_strict     & 0.429 & $-$7.05 & 0.002$^{**}$ \\\\\nrf\\_moderate vs random\\_moderate & 0.242 & $-$3.77 & 0.020$^*$    \\\\\nrf\\_soft vs random\\_soft         & 0.328 & $-$17.98 & ${<}$0.001$^{**}$ \\\\\nrf\\_soft vs unfiltered           & 0.043 &    0.93 & 0.407        \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended_discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation.}\nCurrent data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \\cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.\nRecursive generation depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.\nThe 70--85\\% in-distribution accuracy and $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ suggest depth-graduated filtering is feasible within known model--strategy configurations, though the d1/d2 boundary inversion under cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) means reliable curation requires either known decoding strategy metadata or an upstream strategy-identification step; a small-scale PoC (Appendix~\\ref{app:curation_poc}) shows RF-filtered subsets match unfiltered quality with 44\\% of the data; large-scale validation remains open.\nImportance weighting \\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.\n\n\\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}\nDepth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.\nWatermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.\nSource attribution \\cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.\nDepth estimation characterizes \\emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.\nA layered provenance system combining these three signals---watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth---could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.\n",
      "numLines": 269,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 269
    }
  }
}

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have a thorough understanding of the paper. Let me write a comprehensive adversarial review report. Let me assess the paper from a borderline reviewer's perspective (Rating 5-6).

Key observations:

**Strengths:**
1. Novel task formalization - recursive generation depth estimation is genuinely new
2. Systematic 3×3 grid is thorough
3. EDD metric is well-defined and useful
4. Feature universality finding (p90_ppl, var_surprisal) is compelling
5. Honest reporting of limitations (cross-strategy failure, d0 inflation)


6. The temperature ablation study reveals mechanistic insights about memorization as a prerequisite, and the bibliography is comprehensive with 40+ references
7. End-to-end learning comparison validates that the feature-based approach is sound

**Weaknesses:**

Critical:
1. Scale limitation: Only 1-1.5B models tested, yet the paper claims "universality" across just three models in a narrow parameter range. Modern frontier models are 70B+, and the findings might not generalize where models produce more fluent text.
2. Ecological validity gap: The controlled Wikipedia→LoRA pipeline is far removed from real-world recursive contamination scenarios where models are pretrained rather than fine-tuned, and contamination occurs through messier mechanisms.
3. Cross-strategy transfer essentially fails for practical deployment at 59.1% accuracy, dropping to 45.9% when excluding the baseline. The d1/d2 inversion makes predictions actively misleading when the decoding strategy is unknown.

Major:
1. Reference model circularity: All features come from GPT-2 base (124M), creating architectural overlap when GPT-2 XL is the generator. The holdout GPT-2 XL accuracy of 67.6% isn't lower than OLMo's 69.2%, which is suspicious—the overlap might actually be helping in subtle ways rather than hurting.
2. The "70-85

% accuracy" headline relies heavily on near-perfect d0 classification. The real challenge—distinguishing d1/d2/d3—only reaches 58.8-82.6%, and cross-model performance on d1-d3 drops to just 55.4%.
3. The curation proof-of-concept is underwhelming: RF-based filtering doesn't outperform unfiltered data (p=0.407), so while it avoids the damage of random filtering, it's not actually providing value.
4. Nineteen features with VIF>10 indicates severe multicollinearity, suggesting the effective dimensionality is only 4-5. Reporting all 19 overstates the feature engineering contribution.
5. The EDD=4 claim: Only Pythia shows statistical significance (p=0.001) for d3v4, while OLMo is marginal (p=0.091) and GPT-2 XL is non-significant (p=0.505). The abstract states "EDD_{0.75}=4" without these caveats, even though the paper does qualify them elsewhere.

On the minor side, the zero-shot LLM baselines with DeepSeek and Qwen2.5-7B use a fairly constrained prompt—chain-of-thought or few-shot might improve results. The benchmark relies on Wikipedia as seed data, which is clean and well-formatted, so it's unclear how features would generalize to conversational or informal text. With 180K total samples but only 5K per cell per depth, there's a potential sparsity issue.

The paper also doesn't evaluate instruction-tuned models, which are what people actually use in practice. The Discussion tries to cover too much ground—curation, provenance, mixed-depth, adversarial robustness, scaling—without developing any of these ideas fully.

I'm wondering about several things: whether instruction-tuned models like Llama-2-Chat would behave differently, what happens in a closed-loop scenario where the reference and generation models are the same, whether the feature universality just reflects that all three models are similar in size, how sensitive the pipeline is to the fixed 512-token passage length, and whether the memorization prerequisite means depth estimation fundamentally breaks down at higher temperatures.

To strengthen this work, I'd want to see experiments on at least one 7B model to validate the scaling claims, sensitivity analysis across passage lengths, evaluation on mixed-depth documents, instruction-tuned models as generators, and seed text from domains beyond Wikipedia.
```

{

  "content": "# Adversarial Review Round 1\n\n## Overall Assessment\nRating: 5/10 (Borderline reject)\nConfidence: 4/5\n\n## Summary\nThis paper formalizes recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification, constructs a 3x3 benchmark (3 models x 3 strategies x 4 depths), and presents a \"Depth Degradation Atlas\" characterizing how statistical features evolve across depths. The core finding is that perplexity tail statistics (p90_ppl, var_surprisal) are universal depth markers achieving 70-85% four-class accuracy via Random Forest. The EDD metric quantifies the discriminability frontier. The work is thorough within its controlled scope but raises concerns about ecological validity, scalability, and the gap between the controlled setting and real-world recursive contamination.\n\n## Strengths\n1. **Genuine task novelty.** No prior work estimates ordinal recursive generation depth from text alone. The problem formalization is clean and the EDD metric is a useful contribution that could become standard for this research direction.\n\n2. **Systematic experimental design.** The 3x3 grid with per-class recall reporting, cross-model/cross-strategy LOO, multiple importance measures (SHAP/Gini/permutation with Kendall's W=0.90), and threshold sensitivity analysis demonstrates rigorous methodology. The domain bias analysis (LOGO, seed-group negative control) is particularly commendable.\n\n3. **Feature universality finding is scientifically interesting.** p90_ppl ranking top-2 in 9/9 cells and var_surprisal in 8/9 under SHAP, with mean cross-model Spearman rho=0.868, is a non-trivial finding that constrains the hypothesis space for future work.\n\n4. **Honest limitations reporting.** The paper does not hide the d0 inflation problem, the cross-strategy breakdown, the temperature inversion, or the C4 FPR issues. The d1-d3 numbers are transparently reported alongside headline figures.\n\n5. **End-to-end comparison validates feature approach.** The DeBERTa/RoBERTa comparison with linear probing showing negative R^2 for key features provides a compelling mechanistic explanation for why PLM representations cannot solve this task, not just a performance gap.\n\n6. **Multi-temperature ablation with mechanistic insight.** The \"memorization as prerequisite\" finding (training loss analysis across tau values) is the most scientifically interesting result in the paper—it connects depth discriminability to a fundamental property of the recursive training process.\n\n## Weaknesses (ranked by severity)\n\n### Critical (must fix before submission)\n\n1. **Ecological validity gap undermines practical framing.** The entire benchmark uses Wikipedia seed text with LoRA finetuning (r=16, 3 epochs) and BOS-seeded generation without prompts. Real-world recursive contamination involves: (a) full pretraining or extensive finetuning, (b) prompt-conditioned generation, (c) mixed-source corpora, (d) partial contamination at unknown proportions. The paper frames the contribution as addressing \"web-scraped training corpora increasingly contain multi-generational synthetic content\" (Intro, line 1), yet the experimental setting diverges so far from this scenario that the connection is aspirational rather than demonstrated. The WikiText/C4 calibration experiment (Section 5.5) only tests FPR on human text—it never tests whether the classifier can identify recursively contaminated web text produced under realistic conditions. **The paper should either reframe as a controlled proof-of-concept without practical deployment claims, or provide at least one realistic-pipeline experiment.**\n\n2. **Scale limitation is a fundamental threat to the \"universality\" claim.** Three models (1B-1.5B) with diverse architectures is presented as evidence of universality, but they share a critical property: at this scale, recursive finetuning induces rapid memorization and visible distributional collapse. Modern frontier models (7B-70B+) generate more fluent text with narrower perplexity distributions—the very tail features that dominate here may be compressed below noise at scale. The Discussion paragraph on scaling (lines 42-46 of discussion.tex) is entirely speculative (\"we therefore expect depth estimation to remain feasible\") with no empirical support. Given that this is the paper's primary claim, at minimum one 7B model result is needed to make \"universality\" credible beyond the narrow 1-1.5B scale band.\n\n3. **Cross-strategy transfer failure makes deployment infeasible.** The headline 59.1% drops to 45.9% excluding d0 (chance=33%). The temperature d1/d2 AUC inversion (0.13-0.25) means the classifier actively produces *wrong ordinal rankings* when the strategy is unknown. The proposed mitigation (two-stage pipeline with strategy identification) is entirely hypothetical. This isn't a minor limitation—it means the system cannot be deployed on \"web-scraped training corpora\" where decoding strategy is inherently unknown. The paper needs to either demonstrate the two-stage pipeline works, or significantly narrow its practical claims.\n\n### Major (should fix)\n\n4. **Headline accuracy inflation.** The abstract reports \"70-85% four-class accuracy\" but d0 is trivially separable (>98% recall). The meaningful performance—distinguishing between different depths of synthetic text—is the d1-d3 accuracy: 58.8-82.6% in-distribution, 55.4% cross-model, 45.9% cross-strategy. These should be the headline numbers. The current framing creates an impression of stronger performance than the system delivers for the hard part of the task.\n\n5. **Curation PoC does not support the framing.** The paper frames depth-graduated curation as a practical implication, but Table 9 (Appendix) shows rf_soft vs unfiltered: p=0.407, i.e., RF filtering does NOT improve over using all data unfiltered. The finding is actually \"RF >> random subsampling\" which merely shows that informed selection beats random selection—a trivially true result. The interesting claim would be \"depth filtering improves over unfiltered,\" which is not supported. The Discussion overstates this (\"depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative\") when the PoC shows it enables... approximately the same outcome with less data.\n\n6. **EDD=4 claim requires stronger qualification in the abstract.** The abstract states \"EDD_{0.75}=4\" as if this is a general finding. In reality: Pythia p=0.001 (significant), OLMo p=0.091 (marginal), GPT-2 XL p=0.505 (non-significant). Only 1/3 models achieves statistical significance at the 0.75 threshold for the d3-d4 pair. The body text handles this well (\"graded confidence rather than uniform statistical significance\") but the abstract presents it without qualification. A reader who sees only the abstract gets a misleading impression.\n\n7. **Reference model creates an implicit information leak.** All features are computed via GPT-2 base (124M). When GPT-2 XL generates the text, architectural overlap exists (shared tokenizer, partial weight inheritance from pretraining). The paper notes GPT-2 XL holdout (67.6%) doesn't exceed OLMo (69.2%), but this doesn't rule out more subtle effects—the reference model may be more \"surprised\" by Pythia/OLMo outputs in a way that artificially inflates d0/d1 separation for those models. A sensitivity analysis with a completely independent reference model (e.g., Llama-2-7B) would strengthen the claim.\n\n8. **Feature collinearity inflates complexity.** VIF>10 for 14-16 of 19 features, effective dimensionality ~4-5. The paper acknowledges this but continues to frame the contribution as \"19 candidate features\" throughout. In reality, ~4 independent features are doing the work. The feature table (Table 1) and the presentation should foreground the effective dimensionality rather than the raw count, which creates a misleading impression of design complexity.\n\n### Minor\n\n9. **Zero-shot LLM baselines are under-explored.** n=500 with zero-shot prompting is a weak baseline. No chain-of-thought, no few-shot examples, no feature descriptions in the prompt. DeepSeek V3.2 and Qwen2.5-7B are not state-of-the-art reasoning models. A GPT-4/Claude baseline with 5-shot examples would be a more convincing comparison. The current baseline feels designed to fail.\n\n10. **No instruction-tuned generators tested.** All three models are base models. Instruction-tuned models (Llama-3-8B-Instruct, Mistral-7B-Instruct) dominate real-world generation. Recursive contamination from chat completions may produce different statistical signatures than from base model sampling.\n\n11. **Fixed passage length (512 tokens) is unrealistic.** Real web text varies wildly in length. How sensitive are tail statistics to passage length? At 128 tokens, p90_ppl is computed over ~128 values—the tail estimate becomes noisy. No sensitivity analysis is provided.\n\n12. **The Discussion section is overstuffed.** Mixed-depth documents, adversarial robustness, scaling predictions, provenance toolkit—each gets 1-2 paragraphs of pure speculation. This reads like a \"future work laundry list\" rather than focused discussion of what the results actually mean. Consider cutting or moving to appendix.\n\n13. **Writing: \"Depth Degradation Atlas\" framing.** The Atlas is essentially a set of tables and feature importance rankings across 9 experimental conditions. Calling it an \"Atlas\" creates expectations of richer cartographic metaphors (geographic visualization, navigable maps). The contribution is solid but the branding slightly oversells it.\n\n14. **Decision boundary dilution paragraph.** The observation that binary pair classifiers outperform multi-class classifiers on per-pair AUC is well-known in the ordinal classification literature. Presenting this as a finding rather than a methodological choice slightly misrepresents its novelty.\n\n## Questions for Authors\n\n1. What happens when the reference model IS the same family as the generator (i.e., computing features with Pythia-1.4B on Pythia-1.4B-generated text)? Does self-referential feature computation inflate or deflate performance?\n\n2. The memorization prerequisite finding suggests depth signal requires tau < 1.0. What fraction of real-world API deployments use temperature > 1? If most real generation uses tau in [0.7, 1.0], how does this constrain practical applicability?\n\n3. Could the feature universality across 3 models simply reflect that all share similar parameter count (1-1.5B) and similar pretraining scale? Would you expect the same feature ranking for a 70B model where perplexity distributions are fundamentally narrower?\n\n4. The d2 trough in recall (46-73%) is attributed to feature overlap. But alternatively, is d2 genuinely less distinct because the distributional shift between d1→d2 is smaller than d0→d1 or d2→d3? Can you provide KL divergence between adjacent depth feature distributions to verify?\n\n5. For the curation PoC, why does oracle-strict filtering not improve over unfiltered (p=0.22)? This seems to suggest that removing depth-3 data is actually harmful—contradicting the premise that deep recursive text degrades training. How do you reconcile this?\n\n6. The LOGO analysis uses 10 seed groups. With only 10 folds, statistical power to detect a seed-group effect is limited. Why not 50 or 100 groups?\n\n## Suggested Experiments (prioritized by impact)\n\n1. **One 7B model (e.g., Llama-2-7B or Mistral-7B)**, even with reduced depth chain (d0-d2), to validate whether perplexity tail features retain discriminability at larger scale. This is the single experiment that would most strengthen the universality claim.\n\n2. **Passage length sensitivity analysis**: repeat classification at 128, 256, 512, 1024 tokens to characterize the minimum statistical power needed for reliable depth estimation.\n\n3. **Alternative reference model**: repeat the full pipeline with a non-GPT-2 reference model (e.g., Llama-2-7B) to demonstrate reference-model independence.\n\n4. **Strategy identification experiment**: since the two-stage pipeline is proposed as the deployment solution, demonstrate that decoding strategy can actually be identified from text features alone (even a simple binary nucleus-vs-temperature classifier would help).\n\n5. **Realistic contamination simulation**: mix d0 and d1 text at known proportions (e.g., 90% d0 / 10% d1), then test whether the classifier can identify the contaminated fraction. This would bridge the controlled→ecological gap without requiring full pretraining.\n\n6. **Few-shot LLM baseline**: GPT-4 or Claude with 5-shot examples and chain-of-thought to establish a tighter upper bound on what instruction-following models can achieve.\n"

The Discussion paragraph on scaling (lines 42-46 of discussion.tex) is entirely speculative (\"we therefore expect depth estimation to remain feasible\") with no empirical support. Given that this is the paper's primary claim, at minimum one 7B model result is needed to make \"universality\" credible beyond the narrow 1-1.5B scale band.\n\n3. **Cross-strategy transfer failure makes deployment infeasible.** The headline 59.1% drops to 45.9% excluding d0 (chance=33%). The temperature d1/d2 AUC inversion (0.13-0.25) means the classifier actively produces *wrong ordinal rankings* when the strategy is unknown. The proposed mitigation (two-stage pipeline with strategy identification) is entirely hypothetical. This isn't a minor limitation—it means the system cannot be deployed on \"web-scraped training corpora\" where decoding strategy is inherently unknown. The paper needs to either demonstrate the two-stage pipeline works, or significantly narrow its practical claims.\n\n### Major (should fix)\n\n4. **Headline accuracy inflation.** The abstract reports \"70-85% four-class accuracy\" but d0 is trivially separable (>98% recall). The meaningful performance—distinguishing between different depths of synthetic text—is the d1-d3 accuracy: 58.8-82.6% in-distribution, 55.4% cross-model, 45.9% cross-strategy. These should be the headline numbers. The current framing creates an impression of stronger performance than the system delivers for the hard part of the task.\n\n5. **Curation PoC does not support the framing.** The paper frames depth-graduated curation as a practical implication, but Table 9 (Appendix) shows rf_soft vs unfiltered: p=0.407, i.e., RF filtering does NOT improve over using all data unfiltered. The finding is actually \"RF >> random subsampling\" which merely shows that informed selection beats random selection—a trivially true result. The interesting claim would be \"depth filtering improves over unfiltered,\" which is not supported. The Discussion overstates this (\"depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative\") when the PoC shows it enables... approximately the same outcome with less data.\n\n6. **EDD=4 claim requires stronger qualification in the abstract.** The abstract states \"EDD_{0.75}=4\" as if this is a general finding. In reality: Pythia p=0.001 (significant), OLMo p=0.091 (marginal), GPT-2 XL p=0.505 (non-significant). Only 1/3 models achieves statistical significance at the 0.75 threshold for the d3-d4 pair. The body text handles this well (\"graded confidence rather than uniform statistical significance\") but the abstract presents it without qualification. A reader who sees only the abstract gets a misleading impression.\n\n7. **Reference model creates an implicit information leak.** All features are computed via GPT-2 base (124M). When GPT-2 XL generates the text, architectural overlap exists (shared tokenizer, partial weight inheritance from pretraining). The paper notes GPT-2 XL holdout (67.6%) doesn't exceed OLMo (69.2%), but this doesn't rule out more subtle effects—the reference model may be more \"surprised\" by Pythia/OLMo outputs in a way that artificially inflates d0/d1 separation for those models. A sensitivity analysis with a completely independent reference model (e.g., Llama-2-7B) would strengthen the claim.\n\n8. **Feature collinearity inflates complexity.** VIF>10 for 14-16 of 19 features, effective dimensionality ~4-5. The paper acknowledges this but continues to frame the contribution as \"19 candidate features\" throughout. In reality, ~4 independent features are doing the work. The feature table (Table 1) and the presentation should foreground the effective dimensionality rather than the raw count, which creates a misleading impression of design complexity.\n\n### Minor\n\n9. **Zero-shot LLM baselines are under-explored.** n=500 with zero-shot prompting is a weak baseline. No chain-of-thought, no few-shot examples, no feature descriptions in the prompt. DeepSeek V3.2 and Qwen2.5-7B are not state-of-the-art reasoning models. A GPT-4/Claude baseline with 5-shot examples would be a more convincing comparison. The current baseline feels designed to fail.\n\n10. **No instruction-tuned generators tested.** All three models are base models. Instruction-tuned models (Llama-3-8B-Instruct, Mistral-7B-Instruct) dominate real-world generation. Recursive contamination from chat completions may produce different statistical signatures than from base model sampling.\n\n11. **Fixed passage length (512 tokens) is unrealistic.** Real web text varies wildly in length. How sensitive are tail statistics to passage length? At 128 tokens, p90_ppl is computed over ~128 values—the tail estimate becomes noisy. No sensitivity analysis is provided.\n\n12. **The Discussion section is overstuffed.** Mixed-depth documents, adversarial robustness, scaling predictions, provenance toolkit—each gets 1-2 paragraphs of pure speculation. This reads like a \"future work laundry list\" rather than focused discussion of what the results actually mean. Consider cutting or moving to appendix.\n\n13. **Writing: \"Depth Degradation Atlas\" framing.** The Atlas is essentially a set of tables and feature importance rankings across 9 experimental conditions. Calling it an \"Atlas\" creates expectations of richer cartographic metaphors (geographic visualization, navigable maps). The contribution is solid but the branding slightly oversells it.\n\n14. **Decision boundary dilution paragraph.** The observation that binary pair classifiers outperform multi-class classifiers on per-pair AUC is well-known in the ordinal classification literature. Presenting this as a finding rather than a methodological choice slightly misrepresents its novelty.\n\n## Questions for Authors\n\n1. What happens when the reference model IS the same family as the generator (i.e., computing features with Pythia-1.4B on Pythia-1.4B-generated text)? Does self-referential feature computation inflate or deflate performance?\n\n2. The memorization prerequisite finding suggests depth signal requires tau < 1.0. What fraction of real-world API deployments use temperature > 1? If most real generation uses tau in [0.7, 1.0], how does this constrain practical applicability?\n\n3. Could the feature universality across 3 models simply reflect that all share similar parameter count (1-1.5B) and similar pretraining scale? Would you expect the same feature ranking for a 70B model where perplexity distributions are fundamentally narrower?\n\n4. The d2 trough in recall (46-73%) is attributed to feature overlap. But alternatively, is d2 genuinely less distinct because the distributional shift between d1→d2 is smaller than d0→d1 or d2→d3? Can you provide KL divergence between adjacent depth feature distributions to verify?\n\n5. For the curation PoC, why does oracle-strict filtering not improve over unfiltered (p=0.22)? This seems to suggest that removing depth-3 data is actually harmful—contradicting the premise that deep recursive text degrades training. How do you reconcile this?\n\n6. The LOGO analysis uses 10 seed groups. With only 10 folds, statistical power to detect a seed-group effect is limited. Why not 50 or 100 groups?\n\n## Suggested Experiments (prioritized by impact)\n\n1. **One 7B model (e.g., Llama-2-7B or Mistral-7B)**, even with reduced depth chain (d0-d2), to validate whether perplexity tail features retain discriminability at larger scale. This is the single experiment that would most strengthen the universality claim.\n\n2. **Passage length sensitivity analysis**: repeat classification at 128, 256, 512, 1024 tokens to characterize the minimum statistical power needed for reliable depth estimation.\n\n3. **Alternative reference model**: repeat the full pipeline with a non-GPT-2 reference model (e.g., Llama-2-7B) to demonstrate reference-model independence.\n\n4. **Strategy identification experiment**: since the two-stage pipeline is proposed as the deployment solution, demonstrate that decoding strategy can actually be identified from text features alone (even a simple binary nucleus-vs-temperature classifier would help).\n\n5. **Realistic contamination simulation**: mix d0 and d1 text at known proportions (e.g., 90% d0 / 10% d1), then test whether the classifier can identify the contaminated fraction. This would bridge the controlled→ecological gap without requiring full pretraining.\n\n6. **Few-shot LLM baseline**: GPT-4 or Claude with 5-shot examples and chain-of-thought to establish a tighter upper bound on what instruction-following models can achieve.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [],

    "userModified": false
  }
}
